\begin{split}{\widetilde{A}}_{\mu}^{{(2)}K}&=-\frac{L^{2}}{2}(\nabla^{(0)})^{\nu}F_{\nu\mu}^{{(0)}K},\qquad F^{{(0)}K}={\mathrm{d}}A^{{(0)}K}.\end{split}

(A.16)

Here,
\nabla^{(0)}
is the covariant derivative w.r.t.
g^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}
. The equations of motion also fix the divergence of
A_{\mu}^{{(2)}K}
,

\begin{split}(\nabla^{(0)})^{\mu}A^{{(2)}K}_{\mu}=-\frac{L}{16}({\varepsilon}^{(0)})^{\mu\nu\rho{\sigma}}\sum_{IJ}C_{IJK}F^{{(0)}I}_{\mu\nu}F^{{(0)}J}_{\rho{\sigma}}-\frac{1}{4L}\text{Im}\left[\Phi_{K}^{{(0)}\dagger}{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{(0)}_{K}\right].\\
\end{split}

(A.17)

Here and in the rest of this appendix, Greek indices are raised and lowered w.r.t. the metric
g^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}
.

Finally all coefficients in the metric expansion apart from
g^{(4)}_{\mu\nu}
are fixed by the equations of motion as

\begin{split}g^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}&=-\frac{L^{2}}{2}\left(R^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{6}g^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}R^{(0)}\right),\\
\widetilde{g}^{(4)}_{\mu\nu}&=\frac{1}{4}(T^{{(0)}\text{grav}}_{\mu\nu}+T^{{(0)}\text{gauge}}_{\mu\nu})-\frac{1}{24}g^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}\bigg[\sum_{K=1}^{3}\text{Re}\left[(\Phi_{K}^{(0)})^{\dagger}{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{(0)}_{K}\right]+4\sum_{r=1}^{2}\varphi_{r}^{(0)}\widetilde{\varphi}^{(0)}_{r}\bigg],\\
\widetilde{\widetilde{g}}^{(4)}_{\mu\nu}&=-\frac{1}{48}g^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}\bigg[\sum_{K=1}^{3}|\Phi^{(0)}_{K}|^{2}+4\sum_{r=1}^{2}(\varphi^{(0)}_{r})^{2}\bigg].\end{split}

(A.18)

Here,
T^{{(0)}\text{gauge}}_{\mu\nu}
and
T^{{(0)}\text{grav}}_{\mu\nu}
are the gauge and gravity anomaly stress tensors respectively,

\begin{split}T^{{(0)}\text{grav}}_{\mu\nu}&=L^{4}\bigg[R^{(0)}_{\mu\rho\nu{\sigma}}(R^{(0)})^{\rho{\sigma}}-\frac{1}{4}g^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}(R^{(0)}_{\rho{\sigma}})^{2}-\frac{1}{3}R^{(0)}(R^{(0)})^{\text{TF}}_{\mu\nu}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{6}(\nabla^{(0)}_{\mu}\nabla^{(0)}_{\nu}R^{(0)})^{\text{TF}}+\frac{1}{2}(\nabla^{(0)})^{2}(R^{(0)})^{\text{TF}}_{\mu\nu}\bigg],\\
T^{{(0)}\text{gauge}}_{\mu\nu}&=-L^{2}\sum_{K=1}^{3}\bigg[F^{{(0)}K}_{\mu\rho}(F^{{(0)}K})_{\nu}{}^{\rho}-\frac{1}{4}g^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}(F^{{(0)}K}_{\rho{\sigma}})^{2}\bigg].\end{split}

(A.19)

The superscript TF denotes the trace-free part of the tensor. Both of these stress tensors can be obtained by varying an action

\begin{split}T^{{(0)}\text{grav}}_{\mu\nu}+T^{{(0)}\text{gauge}}_{\mu\nu}&=\frac{2}{\sqrt{-g^{(0)}}}\frac{\delta}{\delta(g^{(0)})^{\mu\nu}}\int_{\partial{\mathcal{M}}}{\mathrm{d}}^{4}y\sqrt{-g^{(0)}}\\
&\qquad\times\bigg[\frac{L^{4}}{4}\bigg((R^{(0)}_{\mu\nu})^{2}-\frac{1}{3}(R^{(0)})^{2}\bigg)-\frac{L^{2}}{4}\sum_{K=1}^{3}(F^{{(0)}K}_{\mu\nu})^{2}\bigg].\end{split}

(A.20)

This action is Weyl invariant so both the stress tensors are traceless (this can be checked explicitly as well). This also implies that
T^{{(0)}\text{grav}}
is conserved whereas
T^{{(0)}\text{gauge}}
satisfies

\begin{split}(\nabla^{(0)})^{\nu}T^{{(0)}\text{gauge}}_{\mu\nu}=-L^{2}\sum_{K=1}^{3}F^{{(0)}K}_{\mu\rho}\nabla^{(0)}_{\nu}(F^{{(0)}K})^{\nu\rho}.\end{split}

(A.21)

In addition to (
A.18
), the equations of motion also fix the trace and divergence of
g^{(4)}
.

\begin{split}\text{tr}[g^{(4)}]&=\frac{1}{4}(g^{(2)}_{\mu\nu})^{2}+\frac{L^{2}}{48}\sum_{K=1}^{3}(F^{{(0)}K}_{\mu\nu})^{2}\\
&-\frac{1}{96}\bigg[\sum_{K=1}^{3}\left(|\Phi^{(0)}_{K}|^{2}+8|\widetilde{\Phi}^{(0)}_{K}|^{2}\right)+4\sum_{r=1}^{2}\left((\varphi^{(0)}_{r})^{2}+8(\widetilde{\varphi}_{r}^{(0)})^{2}\right)\bigg],\\
(\nabla^{(0)})^{\nu}(g^{(4)})^{\text{TF}}_{\mu\nu}&=(\nabla^{(0)})^{\nu}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}[(g^{(0)})^{2}]^{\text{TF}}_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{4}\text{tr}[g^{(2)}](g^{(2)})_{\mu\nu}^{\text{TF}}\bigg]\\
&\qquad-\frac{1}{16}\partial_{\mu}\bigg[\frac{L^{4}}{4}\bigg(R^{(0)}_{\mu\nu})^{2}-\frac{1}{3}(R^{(0)})^{2}\bigg)-\frac{L^{4}}{4}\sum_{K=1}^{3}(F_{\rho{\sigma}}^{{(0)}K})^{2}\bigg]\\
&\qquad-\frac{1}{128}\partial_{\mu}\bigg[\sum_{K=1}^{3}|\Phi^{(0)}_{K}|^{2}+4\sum_{r=1}^{2}(\varphi^{(0)}_{r})^{2}\bigg]\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{8}\sum_{K=1}^{3}F^{{(0)}K}_{\mu\nu}\left[4(A^{{(2)}K})^{\nu}+(\widetilde{A}^{{(2)}K})^{\nu}\right]\\
&\qquad-\frac{1}{32}\bigg[\sum_{K=1}^{3}\text{Re}\left[(\widetilde{\Phi}_{K}^{(0)})^{\dagger}\overleftrightarrow{D^{(0)}_{\mu}}\Phi^{(0)}_{K}\right]+4\sum_{r=1}^{2}\widetilde{\varphi}_{r}^{(0)}\overleftrightarrow{\partial_{\mu}}\varphi^{(0)}_{r}\bigg].\end{split}

(A.22)

A.2
Counterterm action

As mentioned previously, the counterterm action must cancel all the divergences in the on-shell action and at the same time not spoil the variational principle. This is done by taking the counterterm action to depend only on quantities which are intrinsically defined on
\partial{\mathcal{M}}
. In the present case, it turns out that the following counterterm action does the job:

\begin{split}S_{\text{ct}}&=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{5}}\int_{\partial{\mathcal{M}}}{\mathrm{d}}^{4}y\sqrt{-h}\left[-\frac{6}{L}-\frac{L}{2}{\mathcal{R}}-\frac{1}{4L}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{2\log\frac{{\epsilon}}{L}}\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{K=1}^{3}|\Phi_{K}|^{2}+4\sum_{r=1}^{2}\varphi_{r}^{2}\bigg)\right.\\
&\left.\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{L}\log\frac{{\epsilon}}{L}\bigg[\frac{L^{4}}{4}\bigg(({\mathcal{R}}_{\mu\nu})^{2}-\frac{1}{3}{\mathcal{R}}^{2}\bigg)-\frac{L^{2}}{4}\sum_{K=1}^{3}(F_{\mu\nu}^{K})^{2}\bigg]\right],\end{split}

(A.23)

where
{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu\nu}
is the Ricci tensor of
\partial{\mathcal{M}}
and
F_{\mu\nu}
is the induced field strength on
\partial{\mathcal{M}}
. Note that
S_{\text{ct}}
explicitly depends on
\log\frac{{\epsilon}}{L}
(note that the boundary is located at
z={\epsilon}
) which is not covariant. The presence of such terms breaks conformal symmetry and are responsible for the conformal anomaly.

In this section, instead of showing that the full on-shell action is finite with this choice of counterterm action, we will show that the on-shell variation is finite. Importantly, in this calculation, we are no longer imposing Dirichlet boundary conditions on the variations and all divergences must cancel out for all on-shell variations.

Varying the action and imposing equations of motion (and
not
imposing Dirichlet boundary conditions), we find

\begin{split}\delta S_{\text{on-shell}}=\delta S_{\text{grav}}+\delta S_{\text{gauge}}+\delta S_{\text{scalar}},\end{split}

(A.24)

where

\begin{split}\delta S_{\text{grav}}&=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{5}}\int_{\partial{\mathcal{M}}}{\mathrm{d}}^{4}y\sqrt{-h}\delta h^{\mu\nu}\bigg({\mathcal{K}}_{\mu\nu}-h_{\mu\nu}{\mathcal{K}}+\frac{3}{L}h_{\mu\nu}-\frac{L}{2}{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{L}{4}h_{\mu\nu}{\mathcal{R}}\\
&+\frac{1}{8L}h_{\mu\nu}\bigg[1+\frac{1}{2\log\frac{{\epsilon}}{L}}\bigg]\bigg[\sum_{K=1}^{3}|\Phi_{K}|^{2}+4\sum_{r=1}^{2}\varphi_{r}^{2}\bigg]+\frac{1}{2L}\log\frac{{\epsilon}}{L}\left[T^{\text{grav}}_{\mu\nu}+T^{\text{gauge}}_{\mu\nu}\right]\bigg),\\
\delta S_{\text{gauge}}&=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{5}}\int_{\partial{\mathcal{M}}}d^{4}y\sqrt{-h}\sum_{K=1}^{3}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{1}{X^{2}_{K}}(F^{K})^{ab}n_{b}+\frac{1}{6}\sum_{I,J=1}^{3}C_{IJK}{\varepsilon}^{abcde}F^{I}_{bc}A^{J}_{d}n_{e}\bigg)\delta A^{K}_{a}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+L\log\frac{{\epsilon}}{L}\nabla_{\mu}(F^{K})^{\mu\nu}\delta A^{K}_{\nu}\bigg],\\
\delta S_{\text{scalar}}&=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{5}}\int_{\partial{\mathcal{M}}}d^{4}y\sqrt{-h}\sum_{r=1}^{2}\bigg[-\bigg(n^{a}\partial_{a}+\frac{2}{L}\bigg)\varphi_{r}-\frac{\varphi_{r}}{L\log\frac{{\epsilon}}{L}}\bigg]\delta\varphi_{r}\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{16\pi G_{5}}\int_{\partial{\mathcal{M}}}d^{4}y\sqrt{-h}\sum_{K=1}^{3}\bigg[-\frac{1}{4}\text{Re}\bigg[\delta\Phi_{K}^{\dagger}\left(n^{a}D_{a}+\frac{2}{L}\right)\Phi_{K}\bigg]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\bigg(\frac{n^{a}\partial_{a}{\lambda}_{K}}{16(4+{\lambda}_{K})}-\frac{1}{8L\log\frac{{\epsilon}}{L}}\bigg)\delta{\lambda}_{K}\bigg].\end{split}

(A.25)

Plugging in the asymptotic expansions (
A.13
)–(
A.15
) into the above, one can explicitly check that all the divergences cancel on-shell and each of these variations are independently finite in the
{\epsilon}\to 0
limit. Explicitly, these limits work out to be

\begin{split}\delta S_{\text{scalar}}&=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{5}}\int_{\partial{\mathcal{M}}}d^{4}y\sqrt{-g^{(0)}}\bigg(-\frac{1}{4L}\bigg[\sum_{K=1}^{3}\text{Re}\left({\widetilde{\Phi}}^{{(0)}\dagger}_{K}\delta\Phi^{(0)}_{K}\right)+4\sum_{r=1}^{2}\widetilde{\varphi}_{r}^{(0)}\delta\varphi^{(0)}_{r}\bigg]\bigg),\\
\delta S_{\text{gauge}}&=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{5}}\int_{\partial{\mathcal{M}}}d^{4}y\sqrt{-g^{(0)}}\sum_{K=1}^{3}\bigg[\frac{1}{L}\left[2(A^{{(2)}K})^{\mu}+({\widetilde{A}}^{{(2)}K})^{\mu}\right]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{6}\sum_{I,J=1}^{3}C_{IJK}({\varepsilon}^{(0)})^{\mu\nu\rho{\sigma}}F^{{(0)}I}_{\nu\rho}A^{{(0)}J}_{\sigma}\bigg]\delta A^{{(0)}K}_{\mu},\\
\delta S_{\text{grav}}&=\frac{1}{2}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{4}y\sqrt{-g^{(0)}}\langle\,{\mathcal{T}}^{\mu\nu}\,\rangle\delta g^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}\,,\end{split}

(A.26)

where

\begin{split}\langle\,{\mathcal{T}}_{\mu\nu}\,\rangle&=-\lim_{z\to 0}\frac{1}{8\pi G_{5}}\frac{L^{2}}{z^{2}}\bigg[{\mathcal{K}}_{\mu\nu}-h_{\mu\nu}{\mathcal{K}}+\frac{3}{L}h_{\mu\nu}-\frac{L}{2}{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{L}{4}h_{\mu\nu}{\mathcal{R}}\\
&+\frac{1}{8L}h_{\mu\nu}\bigg[1+\frac{1}{2\log\frac{{\epsilon}}{L}}\bigg]\bigg[\sum_{K=1}^{3}|\Phi_{K}|^{2}+4\sum_{r=1}^{2}\varphi_{r}^{2}\bigg]+\frac{1}{2L}\log\frac{{\epsilon}}{L}\left[T^{\text{grav}}_{\mu\nu}+T^{\text{gauge}}_{\mu\nu}\right]\bigg]\\
&=\frac{1}{4\pi G_{5}L}\bigg[(g^{(4)})^{\text{TF}}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{3}{16}T^{{(0)}\text{grav}}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{16}T^{{(0)}\text{gauge}}_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}((g^{(2)})^{2})_{\mu\nu}^{\text{TF}}\\
&+\frac{1}{4}\text{tr}[g^{(2)}](g^{(2)})^{\text{TF}}_{\mu\nu}-g^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}\bigg(\frac{1}{16}\text{tr}[g^{(2)}]^{2}-\frac{1}{16}\text{tr}[(g^{(2)})^{2}]+\frac{L^{2}}{64}\sum_{K=1}^{3}(F^{{(0)}K}_{\mu\nu})^{2}\\
&-\frac{1}{128}\bigg[\sum_{K=1}^{3}|\Phi_{K}^{(0)}|^{2}+4\sum_{r=1}^{2}(\varphi_{r}^{(0)})^{2}\bigg]+\frac{1}{32}\bigg[\sum_{K=1}^{3}\text{Re}\left({\widetilde{\Phi}}_{K}^{{(0)}\dagger}\Phi_{K}^{(0)}\right)+4\sum_{r=1}^{2}\widetilde{\varphi}_{r}^{(0)}\varphi_{r}^{(0)}\bigg]\bigg)\bigg].\end{split}

(A.27)

A.3
VEV of dual CFT operators

AdS/CFT duality conjectures that the bulk partition function with boundary conditions determined by the sources (classically, this is given by the on-shell action) is equal to the CFT partition function where sources couple to their dual operators,

\begin{split}&e^{iS_{\text{on-shell}}\left[g^{(0)},A^{{(0)}K},\Phi^{(0)}_{K},\varphi^{(0)}_{r}\right]}\\
&\qquad=\left\langle e^{i\int{\mathrm{d}}^{4}y\sqrt{-g^{(0)}}\left[\sum\limits_{K=1}^{3}A_{\mu}^{{(0)}K}{\mathcal{J}}_{K}^{\mu}-\frac{1}{64L^{2}}\sum\limits_{K=1}^{3}\left(\Phi_{K}^{{(0)}\dagger}{\cal O}_{\Phi_{K}}+\Phi_{K}^{(0)}{\cal O}_{\Phi_{K}^{\dagger}}\right)-\frac{1}{8L^{2}}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{2}\varphi_{r}^{(0)}{\cal O}_{\varphi_{r}}\right]}\right\rangle_{\text{CFT}}.\end{split}

(A.28)

The metric source
g^{(0)}
describes the background geometry to which the CFT couples. Note also the unusual normalization for the scalar operators.

Differentiating both sides of (
A.28
) w.r.t. the sources, we can find the VEV of all the operators

\begin{split}\left\langle{\mathcal{J}}_{K}^{\mu}\right\rangle_{\text{CFT}}&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g^{(0)}}}\frac{\delta}{\delta A^{{(0)}K}_{\mu}}S_{\text{on-shell}}\left[g^{(0)},A^{{(0)}K},\Phi^{(0)}_{K},\varphi^{(0)}_{r}\right],\\
\left\langle{\cal O}_{\varphi_{r}}\right\rangle_{\text{CFT}}&=-8L^{2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g^{(0)}}}\frac{\delta}{\delta\varphi_{r}^{(0)}}S_{\text{on-shell}}\left[g^{(0)},A^{{(0)}K},\Phi^{(0)}_{K},\varphi^{(0)}_{r}\right],\\
\left\langle{\cal O}_{\Phi_{K}}\right\rangle_{\text{CFT}}&=-64L^{2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g^{(0)}}}\frac{\delta}{\delta\Phi_{K}^{{(0)}\dagger}}S_{\text{on-shell}}\left[g^{(0)},A^{{(0)}K},\Phi^{(0)}_{K},\varphi^{(0)}_{r}\right],\\
\left\langle{\cal O}_{\Phi^{\dagger}_{K}}\right\rangle_{\text{CFT}}&=-64L^{2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g^{(0)}}}\frac{\delta}{\delta\Phi_{K}^{{(0)}}}S_{\text{on-shell}}\left[g^{(0)},A^{{(0)}K},\Phi^{(0)}_{K},\varphi^{(0)}_{r}\right].\end{split}

(A.29)

Using (
A.26
), we can determine the VEV for the
U(1)
currents as

\begin{split}\left\langle{\mathcal{J}}_{K}^{\mu}\right\rangle_{\text{CFT}}&=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{5}L}\bigg[2(A^{{(2)}K})^{\mu}+({\widetilde{A}}^{{(2)}K})^{\mu}-\frac{L}{6}\sum_{I,J=1}^{3}C_{IJK}({\varepsilon}^{(0)})^{\mu\nu\rho{\sigma}}F^{{(0)}I}_{\nu\rho}A^{{(0)}J}_{\sigma}\bigg].\end{split}

(A.30)

The equations of motion (
A.17
) imply a divergence constraint for the current

\begin{split}\nabla^{(0)}_{\mu}\left\langle{\mathcal{J}}_{K}^{\mu}\right\rangle_{\text{CFT}}&=-\frac{1}{32\pi G_{5}L^{2}}\bigg[\text{Im}\left[\Phi_{K}^{{(0)}\dagger}{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{(0)}_{K}\right]+\frac{5L^{2}}{12}({\varepsilon}^{(0)})^{\mu\nu\rho{\sigma}}\sum_{IJ}C_{IJK}F^{{(0)}I}_{\mu\nu}F^{{(0)}J}_{\rho{\sigma}}\bigg].\end{split}

(A.31)

The VEVs of the scalar operators are

\begin{split}\left\langle{\cal O}_{\varphi_{r}}\right\rangle_{\text{CFT}}&=\frac{L}{2\pi G_{5}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{r}^{(0)},\\
\left\langle{\cal O}_{\Phi_{K}}\right\rangle_{\text{CFT}}&=\frac{L}{2\pi G_{5}}{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{{(0)}}_{K},\\
\left\langle{\cal O}_{\Phi^{\dagger}_{K}}\right\rangle_{\text{CFT}}&=\frac{L}{2\pi G_{5}}{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{{(0)}\dagger}_{K}.\\
\end{split}

(A.32)

Finally, differentiating the partition function w.r.t. the background metric
g^{(0)}
we obtain the VEV of the CFT stress tensor (a.k.a. the holographic stress tensor),

\begin{split}\langle\,{\mathcal{T}}_{\mu\nu}\,\rangle_{\text{CFT}}&=\frac{1}{4\pi G_{5}L}\bigg[(g^{(4)})^{\text{TF}}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{3}{16}T^{{(0)}\text{grav}}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{16}T^{{(0)}\text{gauge}}_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}((g^{(2)})^{2})_{\mu\nu}^{\text{TF}}\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{4}\text{tr}[g^{(2)}](g^{(2)})^{\text{TF}}_{\mu\nu}-g^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}\bigg(\frac{1}{16}\text{tr}[g^{(2)}]^{2}-\frac{1}{16}\text{tr}[(g_{\rho{\sigma}}^{(2)})^{2}]\\
&\qquad+\frac{L^{2}}{64}\sum_{K=1}^{3}(F^{{(0)}K}_{\rho{\sigma}})^{2}-\frac{1}{128}\bigg[\sum_{K=1}^{3}|\Phi_{K}^{(0)}|^{2}+4\sum_{r=1}^{2}(\varphi_{r}^{(0)})^{2}\bigg]\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{32}\bigg[\sum_{K=1}^{3}\text{Re}\left({\widetilde{\Phi}}_{K}^{{(0)}\dagger}\Phi_{K}^{(0)}\right)+4\sum_{r=1}^{2}\widetilde{\varphi}_{r}^{(0)}\varphi_{r}^{(0)}\bigg]\bigg)\bigg].\end{split}

(A.33)

Using (
A.22
), we can fix the divergence and trace of the stress tensor

\begin{split}(\nabla^{(0)})^{\nu}\langle\,{\mathcal{T}}_{\mu\nu}\,\rangle_{\text{CFT}}&=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{5}L}\bigg[\sum_{K=1}^{3}F^{{(0)}K}_{\mu\nu}\left[2(A^{{(2)}K})^{\nu}+(\widetilde{A}^{{(2)}K})^{\nu}\right]\\
&\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{K=1}^{3}\text{Re}\left[(\widetilde{\Phi}_{K}^{(0)})^{\dagger}D^{(0)}_{\mu}\Phi^{(0)}_{K}\right]-\sum_{r=1}^{2}\widetilde{\varphi}_{r}^{(0)}\partial_{\mu}\varphi^{(0)}_{r}\bigg],\end{split}

(A.34)

and

\begin{split}\langle\,{\mathcal{T}}^{\mu}{}_{\mu}\,\rangle_{\text{CFT}}&=\frac{L^{3}}{64\pi G_{5}}\bigg[(R^{(0)}_{\mu\nu})^{2}-\frac{1}{3}(R^{(0)})^{2}-\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{K=1}^{3}(F^{{(0)}K}_{\mu\nu})^{2}\bigg]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{32\pi G_{5}L}\bigg[\frac{1}{4}\sum_{K=1}^{3}|\Phi_{K}^{(0)}|^{2}+\sum_{r=1}^{2}(\varphi_{r}^{(0)})^{2}\bigg]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{32\pi G_{5}L}\bigg[\sum_{K=1}^{3}\text{Re}\left({\widetilde{\Phi}}_{K}^{{(0)}\dagger}\Phi_{K}^{(0)}\right)+4\sum_{r=1}^{2}\widetilde{\varphi}_{r}^{(0)}\varphi_{r}^{(0)}\bigg].\end{split}

(A.35)

The last equation is precisely the conformal anomaly in a holographic CFT (with
a=c
). This receives contributions from three sources – one from the background metric, one from the gauge field and one from the scalar fields. The latter is due to the fact that the dual operators have dimension
{\Delta}=2
so their squares have dimension
{\Delta}=4+{\cal O}(1/N^{2})
. In this manuscript, we have turned off all the sources and the background geometry is
{\mathbb{R}}\times S^{3}
so all the anomaly contributions vanish and the stress tensor is exactly conserved. That is to say, with vanishing sources and the holographic dictionary entry (
2.3
), (
A.30
)–(
A.32
) and (
A.33
)–(
A.35
) reduce to
(
3.13
), (
3.12
) and (
3.14
)–(
3.15
) in the single charge truncation of Section
3.2
, and also give the VEVs of the two charge truncation of Section
4.2
.

Appendix B
Perturbative construction of hairy solitons and black holes with
A^{1}=A^{2}\equiv 0,A^{3}\equiv A

In this appendix, we present the explicit solutions for the supersymmetric soliton and hairy black holes obtained in perturbation theory for the single charge truncation. Additional details of the construction which are omitted in Section
3.9
of the main text are also presented here.

B.1
Hairy supersymmetric soliton

Recall that we initiate the perturbative construction with the field expansion (
3.52
).
The equation for the scalar field
\Phi_{(1)}
at
{\cal O}({\epsilon})
is given by

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dr}\left[\frac{r^{3}}{1+r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}\left[(1+r^{2})\Phi_{(1)}(r)\right]\right]=0.\end{split}

(B.1)

The general solution to this equation is

\begin{split}\Phi_{(1)}(r)&=c_{1}\frac{1}{1+r^{2}}+c_{2}\frac{2r^{2}\ln r-1}{r^{2}(1+r^{2})}.\end{split}

(B.2)

c_{1}
(
c_{2}
) denotes the source (response) for the dual scalar operator
{\cal O}_{\Phi}
. The AdS boundary condition (
3.54
) implies that
c_{1}=1
and
c_{2}=0
.

At
{\cal O}({\epsilon}^{2})
, the differential equations for
\varphi_{(2)}
,
A_{(2)}
,
h_{(2)}
and
f_{(2)}
take the form

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dr}\left[\frac{1+r^{2}}{r}\frac{d}{dr}\left[r^{2}\varphi_{(2)}(r)\right]\right]&=\frac{r}{6(1+r^{2})^{2}},\\
\frac{d}{dr}\left[r^{3}\frac{d}{dr}A_{(2)}(r)\right]&=\frac{r^{3}}{(1+r^{2})^{3}},\\
\frac{d}{dr}\left[r^{3}\frac{d}{dr}h_{(2)}(r)\right]&=-\frac{r^{3}}{(1+r^{2})^{3}},\\
\frac{d}{dr}\left[r^{2}f_{(2)}(r)\right]&=\frac{r^{3}}{3(1+r^{2})^{2}}-\frac{1}{3}r^{8}\frac{d}{dr}[r^{-4}h_{(2)}(r)].\end{split}

(B.3)

These equations are solved to

\begin{split}\varphi_{(2)}(r)&=\frac{c_{3}}{r^{2}}+c_{4}\frac{\ln(1+r^{2})}{r^{2}}-\frac{1}{24(1+r^{2})},\\
A_{(2)}(r)&=\mu_{(2)}+\frac{c_{5}}{r^{2}}-\frac{1}{8(1+r^{2})},\\
h_{(2)}(r)&=c_{6}+\frac{c_{7}}{r^{2}}+\frac{1}{8(1+r^{2})},\\
f_{(2)}(r)&=\frac{c_{8}}{r^{2}}+\frac{c_{6}r^{2}}{3}+c_{7}+\frac{r^{2}}{8(1+r^{2})}.\end{split}

(B.4)

AdS boundary conditions (
3.49
) fixes
c_{4}=c_{6}=0
and regularity at
r=0
fixes
c_{3}=c_{5}=c_{7}=c_{8}=0
. The integration constant
\mu_{(2)}
is unfixed at this order. It will be fixed by imposing regularity at
r=0
at
{\cal O}({\epsilon}^{3})
.

At
{\cal O}({\epsilon}^{3})
, the equation for
\Phi_{(3)}
is

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dr}\left[\frac{r^{3}}{1+r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}\left[(1+r^{2})\Phi_{(3)}(r)\right]\right]=-\frac{r^{3}(2-5r^{2}+r^{4})}{2(1+r^{2})^{5}}-\frac{8\mu_{(2)}r^{3}}{(1+r^{2})^{3}}\,.\end{split}

(B.5)

Its solution is

\begin{split}\Phi_{(3)}(r)=c_{9}\frac{1}{1+r^{2}}+c_{10}\frac{2r^{2}\ln r-1}{r^{2}(1+r^{2})}-\frac{r^{2}}{8(1+r^{2})^{3}}-\mu_{(2)}\frac{1+r^{2}\ln(1+\frac{1}{r^{2}})}{r^{2}(1+r^{2})}\,.\end{split}

(B.6)

The AdS boundary condition (
3.54
) fixes
c_{9}=c_{10}=0
and regularity at
r=0
fixes
\mu_{(2)}=0
.

Proceeding in this fashion, we can construct the solution to all orders in
{\epsilon}
. The explicit solution to
{\cal O}({\epsilon}^{15})
can be found in the accompanying
Mathematica
file.

B.2
Hairy black hole at
{\cal O}({\epsilon})

Recall that we initiate the black hole double expansion perturbative construction with the field expansion (
3.63
), (
3.65
) and (
3.68
).
At
{\cal O}(r_{+}^{0})
, the differential equation for
\Phi_{(1,0)}(r)
is given by

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dr}\left[\frac{r^{3}}{1+2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}[(1+2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+r^{2})\Phi_{(1,0)}(r)]\right]=0.\end{split}

(B.7)

The solution satisfying the AdS boundary condition (
3.66
) is given by

\begin{split}\Phi_{(1,0)}=\frac{1}{1+2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+r^{2}}.\end{split}

(B.8)

Using this and moving to
{\cal O}(r_{+}^{2})
, we find the differential equation

\begin{split}&\frac{d}{dr}\left[\frac{r^{3}}{1+2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}[(1+2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+r^{2})\Phi_{(1,2)}(r)]\right]\\
&=\frac{r}{{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}(2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+1+r^{2})^{4}}\left[2r^{4}({\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}(4{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,2)}+2)-1)\right.\\
&\left.\qquad-2r^{2}(2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+1)({\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}(8{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,2)}+2)+1)+4{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}(2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+1)^{2}\right].\end{split}

(B.9)

The solution satisfying the AdS boundary condition (
3.66
) is given by

\begin{split}\Phi_{(1,2)}(r)&=\frac{1}{4{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}r^{2}(2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+1+r^{2})^{2}}\left[-2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}(2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+(4{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,2)}+3)r^{2})\right.\\
&\left.\qquad\qquad\qquad+r^{2}(2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+1+r^{2})\ln\left(1+\frac{2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+1}{r^{2}}\right)+r^{2}+1\right].\end{split}

(B.10)

This describes the solution in the
far-field
region to
{\cal O}(r_{+}^{2})
.

Recalling the near-region radial coordinate (
3.70
),
z=r/r_{+}
, the leading order
near-field
equation is

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dz}\left[z(z^{2}-1)\frac{d}{dz}\Phi_{(1,0)}^{\text{near}}(z)\right]=0.\end{split}

(B.11)

The general solution to this equation is given by

\begin{split}\Phi_{(1,0)}^{\text{near}}(z)=c_{1}+c_{2}\ln\frac{z^{2}}{z^{2}-1}.\end{split}

(B.12)

Regularity at the horizon
z=1
requires that
c_{2}=0
. Using this and moving to
{\cal O}(r_{+}^{2})
, we find the equation

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dz}\left[z(z^{2}-1)\frac{d}{dz}\Phi_{(1,2)}^{\text{near}}(z)\right]=-\frac{8c_{1}z^{3}}{1+2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}}.\end{split}

(B.13)

The general solution is

\begin{split}\Phi_{(1,2)}^{\text{near}}(z)=c_{3}+c_{4}\ln\frac{z^{2}}{z^{2}-1}-\frac{c_{1}}{1+2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}}\left(z^{2}+\ln\frac{r_{+}^{2}z^{2}}{1+2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}}\right).\end{split}

(B.14)

Regularity at
z=1
sets
c_{3}=0
.

The integration constants
c_{1}
and
c_{3}
are fixed by matching the near- and far-field solutions. Let us now describe this procedure. We start by setting
r=zr_{+}
in the far-field solution and then expand the solution at small
z
and
r_{+}
. We find

\begin{split}\Phi_{(1)}(zr_{+},r_{+})&=\Phi_{(1,0)}(zr_{+})+r_{+}^{2}\Phi_{(1,2)}(zr_{+})+{\cal O}(r_{+}^{4})\\
&=\left[\frac{1-2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}}{4{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}(2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+1)z^{2}}+\frac{1}{2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+1}+{\cal O}(z^{4})\right]\\
&\qquad+r_{+}^{2}\left[\frac{-8{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,2)}{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}-2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}-1-(2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+1)\ln\frac{r^{2}_{+}z^{2}}{2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+1}}{4{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}(2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+1)^{2}}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{z^{2}}{(2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+1)^{2}}+{\cal O}(z^{4})\right]+{\cal O}(r_{+}^{4}).\end{split}

(B.15)

We next expand the near-field solution at large
z
,

\begin{split}\Phi^{\text{near}}_{(1)}(z,r_{+})&=\Phi^{\text{near}}_{(1,0)}(z)+r_{+}^{2}\Phi^{\text{near}}_{(1,2)}(z)+{\cal O}(r_{+}^{4})\\
&=c_{1}+r_{+}^{2}\left[-\frac{c_{1}z^{2}}{2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+1}+c_{3}-\frac{c_{1}\ln\frac{r_{+}^{2}z^{2}}{2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+1}}{2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}+1}+{\cal O}(z^{-4})\right]+{\cal O}(r_{+}^{4}).\end{split}

(B.16)

The expansion (
B.15
) is valid when
r\ll 1
or
z\ll\frac{1}{r_{+}}
whereas the expansion (
B.16
) is valid when
z\gg 1
. When
r_{+}\ll 1
, the two expansions have an overlapping region of validity and we can match the expansions exactly in this region. It is clear that the matching requires us to set

\begin{split}{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}=\frac{1}{2},\qquad c_{1}=\frac{1}{2},\qquad c_{3}=-\frac{1}{4}(1+2{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,2)}).\end{split}

(B.17)

Note that after this matching we can replace
{\widetilde{q}}_{(0,0)}=\frac{1}{2}
back in (
B.7
) or (
B.9
) to get the ODE (
3.69
) that we present in the main text (see also the associated footnote
27
).

Proceeding in this fashion, we can construct the solution to all orders in
r_{+}^{2}
. The solution to
{\cal O}(r_{+}^{8})
is given in the
Mathematica
file.

B.3
Hairy black hole at
{\cal O}({\epsilon}^{2})

As mentioned in section
3.9.2
, the perturbative construction of the single charge hairy black hole solution is intricate due to the fact that the solution is constructed as a perturbation around a singular solution, namely the singular supersymmetric soliton (
3.24
). The first indication of these intricacies show up at
{\cal O}({\epsilon}^{2})
in perturbation theory and we describe these in this section. We will find that this complicates the perturbative construction significantly. We leave a resolution of the issues discussed here for future work.

The issue arises since we are effectively perturbing around the singular soliton. To understand this, we strip off all the complications of the BCS black hole solution and consider a simpler perturbative expansion around the singular soliton. In the gauge (
3.42
), the general singular soliton (
3.24
) is given by

\begin{split}f=1+r^{2}h,\qquad A_{t}=\frac{1}{h},\qquad\Phi=\frac{\sqrt{C_{2}^{2}-4(1+C_{1})}}{1+r^{2}h},\qquad\varphi=-\frac{1}{3}\ln h,\end{split}

(B.18)

where

\begin{split}h=\sqrt{1+\frac{C_{2}}{r^{2}}+\frac{1+C_{1}}{r^{4}}}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,.\end{split}

(B.19)

The regular solution occurs for
C_{1}=0
. The
r_{+}\to 0
limit of the BCS black hole is the singular soliton satisfying
C_{2}=2\sqrt{1+C_{1}}=2+4{\widetilde{q}}\equiv 2\eta
. The hairy BH solution is a perturbation around this singular solution with
{\widetilde{q}}=\frac{1}{2}\Rightarrow\eta=2
whereas the regular supersymmetric soliton solution is a perturbation around vacuum AdS with
{\widetilde{q}}=0\Rightarrow\eta=1
. We consider the perturbative expansion for arbitrary
\eta
to illustrate the key differences between the two cases.

To initiate the perturbative expansion, we set

\begin{split}f(r,{\epsilon})&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\epsilon}^{2n}f_{(2n)}(r),\\
h(r,{\epsilon})&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\epsilon}^{2n}h_{(2n)}(r),\\
A_{t}(r,{\epsilon})&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\epsilon}^{2n}A_{(2n)}(r),\\
\varphi(r,{\epsilon})&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\epsilon}^{2n}\varphi_{(2n)}(r),\\
\Phi(r,{\epsilon})&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\epsilon}^{2n+1}\Phi_{(2n+1)}(r),\end{split}

(B.20)

where

\begin{split}f_{(0)}=1+r^{2}h_{(0)},\qquad A_{(0)}=\frac{1}{h_{(0)}},\qquad\varphi_{(0)}=-\frac{1}{3}\ln h_{(0)},\qquad h_{(0)}=1+\frac{\eta-1}{r^{2}}.\end{split}

(B.21)

We plug this into the equations (
3.44
)–(
3.48
) and solve them order-by-order in
{\epsilon}
. At
{\cal O}({\epsilon})
, only the scalar equation is non-trivial and the solution satisfying the AdS boundary condition is given by

\begin{split}\Phi_{(1)}(r)=\frac{1}{\eta+r^{2}}.\end{split}

(B.22)

This precisely matches the solution (
B.8
) obtained at
{\cal O}({\epsilon}r_{+}^{0})
. We use this solution and move on to
{\cal O}({\epsilon}^{2})
. Here, we find it convenient to define new functions

\begin{split}\varphi_{(2)}(r)&\equiv X_{(2)}(r)+\frac{r^{2}(\eta-1+3r^{2})h_{(2)}(r)}{6(\eta-1)(\eta-1+r^{2})}-\frac{[r^{-1}[r^{4}f_{(2)}(r)]^{\prime}]^{\prime}}{16(\eta-1)r},\\
h_{(2)}(r)&\equiv Y_{(2)}(r)-\frac{[r^{4}f_{(2)}(r)]^{\prime}}{2r^{5}},\\
A_{(2)}(r)&\equiv Z_{(2)}(r)+\frac{r^{6}(\eta+1+r^{2})Y_{(2)}(r)}{2(\eta-1)(\eta-1+r^{2})^{2}}-\frac{r^{4}(\eta+r^{2})}{8(\eta-1)(\eta-1+r^{2})}f_{(2)}^{\prime\prime}(r)\\
&\qquad-\frac{r^{3}[3\eta^{2}+\eta+r^{4}+(4\eta-1)r^{2}-4]}{8(\eta-1)(\eta-1+r^{2})^{2}}f_{(2)}^{\prime}(r)\\
&\qquad+\frac{r^{2}\left(-2\eta+r^{4}+(\eta+1)r^{2}+2\right)}{2(\eta-1)(\eta-1+r^{2})^{2}}f_{(2)}(r).\end{split}

(B.23)

Note that these definitions are valid only if
\eta\neq 1
. The
\eta=1
background is vacuum
{\text{AdS}}_{5}
and the corresponding perturbative construction reproduces the regular hairy supersymmetric soliton as described in (
3.9.1
). In any case, we are interested in the case
\eta=2
. The differential equations for
X_{(2)}
,
Y_{(2)}
and
Z_{(2)}
take a particularly simple form,

\begin{split}X_{(2)}(r)&=\frac{r^{2}(r^{2}+2\eta)}{16(\eta-1)(r^{2}+\eta)^{3}},\\
\left[\frac{1}{r}[r^{4}Y_{(2)}(r)]^{\prime}\right]^{\prime}&=-\frac{r^{3}}{(\eta+r^{2})^{3}}-8(\eta-1)X_{(2)}^{\prime}(r),\\
Z_{(2)}^{\prime}(r)&=\frac{4r(\eta-1)X_{(2)}(r)}{(\eta-1+r^{2})}-\frac{2r^{2}(\eta+r^{2})X_{(2)}^{\prime}(r)}{\eta-1+r^{2}}-\frac{r^{5}}{4(\eta-1)(\eta+r^{2})^{3}}.\end{split}

(B.24)

X_{(2)}
satisfies an algebraic equation which has already been solved above. The last two differential equations are easily integrated up to 3 integration constants.

\begin{split}Y_{(2)}(r)&=\frac{c_{1}}{r^{2}}+\frac{c_{2}}{r^{4}}+\frac{\eta}{8r^{2}(\eta+r^{2})^{2}},\\
Z_{(2)}(r)&=c_{3}+\frac{(\eta+1)r^{4}}{8(\eta-1)(\eta+r^{2})^{2}(\eta-1+r^{2})}.\end{split}

(B.25)

Using these solutions, we then find a 4th order differential equation for
f_{(2)}
. It is convenient to write

\begin{split}f_{(2)}(r)=-c_{1}-\frac{1}{8(\eta+r^{2})}+{\widetilde{f}}_{(2)}(r).\end{split}

(B.26)

{\widetilde{f}}_{(2)}(r)
satisfies a homogeneous differential equation

\begin{split}0&=r^{3}(\eta+r^{2}){\widetilde{f}}_{(2)}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}(r)+2r^{2}(6r^{2}+5\eta){\widetilde{f}}_{(2)}^{\prime\prime\prime}(r)+r(29r^{2}+23\eta-8){\widetilde{f}}_{(2)}^{\prime\prime}(r)\\
&\qquad\qquad+(-13r^{2}+9\eta-24){\widetilde{f}}_{(2)}^{\prime}(r)-32r{\widetilde{f}}_{(2)}(r).\end{split}

(B.27)

The general solution to this equation is

\begin{split}{\widetilde{f}}_{(2)}(r)&=\frac{c_{4}}{r^{2}}+c_{5}(2\eta-2+r^{2})+c_{6}{\gamma}_{+}(r)+c_{7}{\gamma}_{-}(r),\end{split}

(B.28)

where

\begin{split}{\gamma}_{\pm}(r)\equiv r^{-1\pm{\alpha}}\,_{2}F_{1}\left(\frac{1\pm{\alpha}}{2},\frac{-3\pm{\alpha}}{2};1\pm{\alpha};-\frac{r^{2}}{\eta}\right),\qquad{\alpha}\equiv\sqrt{1+8/\eta}.\end{split}

(B.29)

We immediately notice a qualitative different structure for the solution depending on whether
{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{N}}
or not. When
{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the hypergeometric reduces to a simple rational function of
r^{2}
. Consequently, in this case the solution for all
\varphi_{(2)}
,
h_{(2)}
,
A_{(2)}
are all rational functions of
r^{2}
and asymptotically AdS boundary conditions are easy to impose. Further, we recall that the solutions constructed here are valid in the
far-field
region. To match the solutions to the near-field expansion at small
r
, we set
r=r_{+}z
and expand in small
z
. Since, the functions are rational functions of
r^{2}
, only terms of the form
{\cal O}(r^{2n})
appear in its small
r
expansion. Such terms are matched to terms of the form
{\cal O}(r_{+}^{2n}z^{2n})
in the large
z
expansion of the near-field solution. This process is identical to the one described for the scalar field
\Phi_{(1)}
at
{\cal O}({\epsilon})
in section
B.2
. Note that this is exactly what happens for
\eta=1\Rightarrow{\alpha}=3\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

On the other hand, the hairy BH is a perturbation around the
\eta=2\Rightarrow{\alpha}=\sqrt{5}\not\in{\mathbb{N}}
singular soliton. In this case,
{\gamma}_{\pm}(r)
have problematic terms in their expansion near
r=\infty
and
r=0
. It’s expansion near
r=\infty
is

\begin{split}{\gamma}_{\pm}(r)~~\propto~~\frac{{\alpha}^{2}-1}{{\alpha}^{2}-9}r^{2}-2+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left[3-4H_{\frac{1}{2}(\pm{\alpha}-1)}+2\ln\frac{r^{2}}{\eta}\right]+{\cal O}(r^{-4}).\end{split}

(B.30)

The
\log
term in this expansion violates the AdS boundary conditions (
3.49
). The resolution for this is simple - we simply choose the integration constants
c_{6}
and
c_{7}
so that the
\log
terms cancel out from
f_{(2)}(r)
.

Near
r=0
, the hypergeometric function admits an expansion of the form

\begin{split}{\gamma}_{\pm}(r)=r^{-1\pm{\alpha}}[1+{\cal O}(r^{2})].\end{split}

(B.31)

Such terms are matched to terms of the form
{\cal O}(r_{+}^{-1\pm{\alpha}}z^{-1\pm{\alpha}})
in the large
z
expansion of the near-field solution. However, this immediately implies that the near-field solution
does not
admit an analytic expansion in
r_{+}^{2}
, e.g. the scalar field at
{\cal O}({\epsilon})
in (
3.72
). A possible resolution to this is to set both
c_{6}=c_{7}=0
. The problem is that choice may clash with the imposition of AdS boundary conditions and this is indeed what happens at higher orders in perturbation theory (in the construction of the hairy BH, this first shows up at
{\cal O}({\epsilon}^{2}r_{+}^{4})
). At this order in perturbation theory and beyond, AdS boundary conditions will force at least one of
c_{6}
or
c_{7}
(or both) to be non-vanishing. This will then force us to introduce to non-analytic terms in the near-field expansion of
f_{(2)}
. A general small
r_{+}
expansion for
f_{(2)}
then takes the form

\begin{split}f_{(2)}^{\text{near}}(z,r_{+})=\sum_{m,n,p=0}^{\infty}r_{+}^{2p+m(-1+{\alpha})+n(-1-{\alpha})}f_{(2,2p,2m,2n)}^{\text{near}}(z).\end{split}

(B.32)

Similar expansions will also exist for
h_{(2)}
,
\varphi_{(2)}
and
A_{(2)}
. However, now, the presence of such terms in the near-field expansion will backreact and introduce similar non-analytic terms in the far-field expansion as well so we will have to modify that expansion as well. Of course, it will further backreact on to the scalar field at
{\cal O}({\epsilon}^{3})
.

Another complication arises due to the fact that
{\alpha}+1=\sqrt{5}+1>2
which implies that the far-field expansion actually breaks down when
r\sim r_{+}^{\frac{1}{2}(\sqrt{5}-1)}
which for small
r_{+}
is parametrically larger than
r_{+}
. Consequently, we would need to introduce a new intermediate-field expansion to construct the solution in this region.

Based on the discussion in this section, it is clear that the construction of the hairy BH solution is significantly more intricate than previously presumed. We hope to resolve these issues in future work.

Explicit Results for the hairy BH solution at
{\cal O}({\epsilon}^{2})

Having the described the issue qualitatively in the previous section, we present explicit results up to
{\cal O}({\epsilon}^{2}r_{+}^{4})
at which point the perturbative construction breaks down.

We start by plugging in the expansion (
3.63
) into equations (
3.44
)–(
3.47
) and extracting the equations at
{\cal O}({\epsilon}^{2})
. To solve these equations, we further expand in
r_{+}

\begin{split}f_{(2)}(r,r_{+})&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r_{+}^{2n}f_{(2,2n)}(r),\\
h_{(2)}(r,r_{+})&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r_{+}^{2n}h_{(2,2n)}(r),\\
A_{(2)}(r,r_{+})&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r_{+}^{2n}A_{(2,2n)}(r),\\
\varphi_{(2)}(r,r_{+})&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r_{+}^{2n}\varphi_{(2,2n)}(r).\end{split}

(B.33)

The differential equations at each order in
r_{+}^{2}
take a simpler form if we work instead with the functions
X_{(2,2n)}
,
Y_{(2,2n)}
and
Z_{(2,2n)}
which are defined as in (
B.23
) with
\eta=2
. The differential equations these functions and
f_{(2,2n)}
have exactly the same form as (
B.24
) and (
B.27
), now with
\eta=2
and additional source terms. At
{\cal O}(r_{+}^{0})
, the solutions are

\begin{split}X_{(2,0)}(r)&=\frac{r^{2}(4+r^{2})}{16(2+r^{2})^{3}},\\
Y_{(2,0)}(r)&=\frac{c_{1}}{r^{2}}+\frac{c_{2}}{r^{4}}-\frac{4+r^{2}}{16(2+r^{2})^{2}},\\
Z_{(2,0)}(r)&=c_{3}+\frac{1+r^{2}}{2(2+r^{2})^{2}},\\
f_{(2,0)}(r)&=c_{4}(2+r^{2})+\frac{c_{5}}{r^{2}}-c_{1}+\frac{r^{2}}{16(2+r^{2})}.\end{split}

(B.34)

Here we have set
c_{6}=c_{7}=0
as this is consistent with AdS boundary conditions. Using this, we find

\begin{split}\varphi_{(2,0)}(r)&=\frac{c_{2}+c_{5}}{6r^{2}}+\frac{\frac{1}{16}+c_{1}+c_{2}-c_{4}+c_{5}}{3(1+r^{2})}-\frac{1}{24(2+r^{2})},\\
h_{(2,0)}(r)&=3c_{4}-\frac{c_{1}-4c_{4}}{r^{2}}+\frac{c_{2}+c_{5}}{r^{4}}+\frac{1}{16(2+r^{2})},\\
A_{(2,0)}(r)&=c_{3}+\frac{2r^{6}+7r^{4}+10r^{2}+4}{16(r^{2}+1)^{2}(r^{2}+2)}\\
&\qquad\qquad+\frac{(2c_{1}+2c_{5}+3c_{2}-4c_{4})r^{2}+(c_{2}-2c_{4})r^{4}}{2(1+r^{2})^{2}},\\
f_{(2,0)}(r)&=c_{4}(2+r^{2})+\frac{c_{5}}{r^{2}}-c_{1}+\frac{r^{2}}{16(2+r^{2})}.\end{split}

(B.35)

AdS boundary conditions imply
c_{4}=0
. All other constants are fixed by matching with the near-field solution.

To construct the solution in the near-field region, we define

\begin{split}f_{(2)}^{\text{near}}(z,r_{+})&=f_{(2)}(zr_{+},r_{+}),\\
h_{(2)}^{\text{near}}(z,r_{+})&=h_{(2)}(zr_{+},r_{+}),\\
A_{(2)}^{\text{near}}(z,r_{+})&=A_{(2)}(zr_{+},r_{+}),\\
\varphi_{(2)}^{\text{near}}(z,r_{+})&=\varphi_{(2)}(zr_{+},r_{+}).\\
\end{split}

(B.36)

Note that as
r_{+}\to 0
, the base solution has the behaviour

\begin{split}f_{(0)}^{\text{near}}(z,r_{+})&={\cal O}(r_{+}^{0}),\\
h_{(0)}^{\text{near}}(z,r_{+})&={\cal O}(r_{+}^{-2}),\\
A_{(0)}^{\text{near}}(z,r_{+})&={\cal O}(r_{+}^{2}),\\
\varphi_{(0)}^{\text{near}}(z,r_{+})&={\cal O}(r_{+}^{0}).\end{split}

(B.37)

Consequently, the small
r_{+}
expansion of the fields are

\begin{split}f^{\text{near}}_{(2)}(z,r_{+})&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r_{+}^{2n}f^{\text{near}}_{(2,2n)}(z),\\
h^{\text{near}}_{(2)}(z,r_{+})&=\frac{1}{r_{+}^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r_{+}^{2n}h^{\text{near}}_{(2,2n)}(z),\\
A^{\text{near}}_{(2)}(z,r_{+})&=r_{+}^{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r_{+}^{2n}A^{\text{near}}_{(2,2n)}(z),\\
\varphi^{\text{near}}_{(2)}(z,r_{+})&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r_{+}^{2n}\varphi^{\text{near}}_{(2,2n)}(z).\end{split}

(B.38)

The differential equations at each order in
r_{+}^{2}
takes a simpler form if we define

\begin{split}X^{\text{near}}_{(2,2n)}(z)&\equiv\varphi^{\text{near}}_{(2,2n)}(z)+\frac{1}{8}z^{1/3}[z^{-7/3}[z^{8/3}h^{\text{near}}_{(2,2n)}(z)]]^{\prime},\\
Y^{\text{near}}_{(2,2n)}(z)&\equiv h^{\text{near}}_{(2,2n)}(z)-\frac{1}{2z^{5}}[z^{4}f_{(2,2n)}^{\text{near}}(z)]^{\prime},\\
Z^{\text{near}}_{(2,2n)}(z)&\equiv A^{\text{near}}_{(2,2n)}(z)+\frac{1}{2z}[z^{4}f_{(2,2n)}^{\text{near}}(z)]^{\prime}.\end{split}

(B.39)

The differential equations then take the form

\begin{split}{\mathscr{S}}_{(2,2n)}^{X,{\text{near}}}(z)&=[X_{(2,2n)}^{\text{near}}(z)]^{\prime},\\
{\mathscr{S}}_{(2,2n)}^{Y,{\text{near}}}(z)&=[z^{4}Y_{(2,2n)}^{\text{near}}(z)]^{\prime}-8yX_{(2,2n)}^{\text{near}}(z),\\
{\mathscr{S}}_{(2,2n)}^{Z,{\text{near}}}(z)&=[z^{-1}[Z_{(2,2n)}^{\text{near}}(z)]^{\prime}]^{\prime},\\
{\mathscr{S}}^{f,{\text{near}}}_{(2,2n)}(z)&=z^{2}(z^{2}-1)[f_{(2,2n)}^{\text{near}}(z)]^{\prime\prime\prime}+7y(z^{2}-1)[f_{(2,2n)}^{\text{near}}(z)]^{\prime\prime}\\
&\qquad+5(z^{2}-1)[f_{(2,2n)}^{\text{near}}(z)]^{\prime}-8zf_{(2,2n)}^{\text{near}}(z)+4[Z_{(2,2n)}^{\text{near}}(z)]^{\prime}\\
&\qquad+2[z(z^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{2}}[z^{2}(z^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{(2,2n)}^{\text{near}}(z)]^{\prime}]^{\prime}\\
&\qquad-16(z^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{2}}[(z^{2}-1)^{\frac{1}{2}}X_{(2,2n)}^{\text{near}}(z)]^{\prime}.\end{split}

(B.40)

As always, the sources are fixed by lower orders in perturbation theory. It is easy to integrate these differential equations and obtain the solution up to 7 integration constants. The general solution has the form

\begin{split}\varphi^{\text{near}}_{(2,2n)}(z)&=\frac{4b_{1}-b_{4}}{6}+\frac{b_{2}+b_{5}}{6z^{2}}+\text{source},\\
h^{\text{near}}_{(2,2n)}(z)&=\frac{b_{2}+b_{5}}{z^{4}}+\frac{2(2b_{1}+b_{4})}{z^{2}}+\text{source},\\
A^{\text{near}}_{(2,2n)}(z)&=b_{3}-b_{5}-b_{4}z^{2}+\text{source},\\
f_{(2,2n)}^{\text{near}}(z)&=\frac{b_{5}}{z^{2}}-\frac{b_{2}+b_{5}}{z^{4}}+b_{4}+b_{6}\rho_{+}(z)+b_{7}\rho_{-}(z)+\text{source},\end{split}

(B.41)

where the “source” terms are obtained by integration the sources
{\mathscr{S}}
in (
B.40
) and

\begin{split}\rho_{\pm}(z)=(z^{2}-1)^{-\frac{1}{2}(\pm\sqrt{5}+1)}\,_{2}F_{1}\left(\frac{1\pm\sqrt{5}}{2},\frac{5\pm\sqrt{5}}{2};1\pm\sqrt{5};\frac{1}{1-z^{2}}\right).\end{split}

(B.42)

The hypergeometric functions
\rho_{\pm}
are the near-field analogue of the far-field hypergeometrics (
B.29
). Their presence in the near-field solution would imply non-analytic terms in the small
r_{+}
expansion of the far-field solution and this is precisely what we expect will happen at a sufficiently high order.

At
{\cal O}(r_{+}^{0})
, all the near-field sources are identically zero and we find the solution

\begin{split}\varphi_{(2,0)}^{\text{near}}(z)&=\frac{4b_{1}-b_{4}}{6}+\frac{b_{2}+b_{5}}{6z^{2}},\\
h_{(2,0)}^{\text{near}}(z)&=\frac{2(2b_{1}+b_{4})}{z^{2}}+\frac{b_{2}+b_{5}}{z^{4}},\\
A_{(2,0)}^{\text{near}}(z)&=b_{3}-b_{5}-b_{4}z^{2},\\
f_{(2,0)}^{\text{near}}(z)&=-\frac{b_{2}}{z^{4}}+b_{4}+b_{5}\frac{z^{2}-1}{z^{4}}.\end{split}

(B.43)

Here, we have consistently set
b_{6}=b_{7}=0
.
f
and
A
must vanish on the horizon
z=1
. This implies
b_{4}=b_{2}
and
b_{5}=b_{3}-b_{2}
. The remaining constants are fixed by matching to the far-field solution (
B.35
) in the usual way. As a result of the matching, we have
c_{2}=c_{5}=b_{1}=b_{2}=0
and
c_{3}=-\frac{1}{8}
. The remaining unfixed integration constants are
c_{1}
and
b_{3}
.
b_{3}=x_{(2,0)}
will will be fixed at
{\cal O}(r_{+}^{2})
in perturbation theory. On the other hand,
c_{1}
is a redundant integration constant. In the full solution this constant appears alongside
{\widetilde{q}}_{(2,0)}
in the combination
c_{1}-2{\widetilde{q}}_{(2,0)}
. Since only this combination appears in the final solution, we can set
c_{1}=0
without loss of generality.

The far- and near-field solution at
{\cal O}(r_{+}^{2})
is given by

\begin{split}\varphi_{(2,2)}(r)&=-\frac{r^{2}\log\left(\frac{2}{r^{2}}+1\right)}{48(r^{2}+1)(r^{2}+2)}+\frac{-r^{6}+5r^{4}+8r^{2}}{192(r^{4}+3r^{2}+2)^{2}}\\
h_{(2,2)}(r)&=\frac{r^{2}-4}{64(r^{2}+2)^{2}}+\frac{\log\left(\frac{2}{r^{2}}+1\right)}{16(r^{2}+2)},\\
A_{(2,2)}(r)&=-\frac{r^{4}\log\left(\frac{2}{r^{2}}+1\right)}{16(r^{2}+1)^{2}(r^{2}+2)}+\frac{-r^{8}+11r^{6}+24r^{4}+8r^{2}}{64(r^{2}+1)^{3}(r^{2}+2)^{2}},\\
f_{(2,2)}(r)&=\frac{(r^{2}-4)r^{2}}{64(r^{2}+2)^{2}}+\frac{r^{2}\log\left(\frac{2}{r^{2}}+1\right)}{16(r^{2}+2)},\\
\end{split}

(B.44)

\begin{split}\varphi_{(2,2)}^{\text{near}}(z)&=\frac{x_{(2,2)}}{6z^{2}}-\frac{z^{2}}{96},\\
h_{(2,2)}^{\text{near}}(z)&=\frac{x_{(2,2)}}{z^{4}}+\frac{1}{32},\\
A_{(2,2)}^{\text{near}}(z)&=-\frac{1}{32}z^{2}\left(z^{2}-1\right),\\
f_{(2,2)}^{\text{near}}(z)&=\frac{\left(z^{2}-1\right)\left(32x_{(2,2)}+z^{4}+z^{2}\right)}{32z^{4}}.\end{split}

(B.45)

The matching of solutions also sets
x_{(2,0)}=0
.

Finally, we turn to the solution at
{\cal O}(r_{+}^{4})
. The far-field solution has the form

\begin{split}X_{(2,4)}(r)&=\frac{r^{2}}{384\left(r^{2}+1\right)^{3}\left(r^{2}+2\right)^{5}}\left[55r^{10}+607r^{8}+2779r^{6}+5207r^{4}+4214r^{2}\right.\\
&\qquad\qquad\left.+12(r^{2}+1)^{3}(r^{2}+2)^{2}(r^{2}+4)\ln^{2}\left(\frac{2}{r^{2}}+1\right)\right.\\
&\qquad\qquad\left.+4\left(r^{2}+1\right)\left(r^{2}+2\right)\left(2r^{6}+24r^{4}+39r^{2}+16\right)\ln\frac{r_{+}^{2}}{2}\right.\\
&\qquad\qquad\left.-2(r^{2}+1)(r^{2}+2)(15r^{8}+148r^{6}+531r^{4}\right.\\
&\qquad\qquad\left.+672r^{2}+272)\ln\left(\frac{2}{r^{2}}+1\right)+1232\right],\\
Y_{(2,4)}(r)&=\frac{c_{2}}{r^{2}}+c_{1}+\frac{1}{64r^{2}(r^{2}+2)^{4}}\left[r^{2}\left((r^{2}+2)\ln\left(\frac{2}{r^{2}}+1\right)\left(5r^{6}+38r^{4}\right.\right.\right.\\
&\left.\left.\left.\qquad\qquad+84r^{2}-2\left(r^{4}+6r^{2}+8\right)r^{2}\ln\left(\frac{2}{r^{2}}+1\right)+8\right)\right.\right.\\
&\left.\left.\qquad\qquad-4(r^{4}-4)\ln\frac{r_{+}^{2}}{2}\right)-r^{2}(10r^{6}+77r^{4}+192r^{2}+56)-16\right],\\
Z_{(2,4)}(r)&=c_{3}-\frac{1}{192(r^{4}+3r^{2}+2)^{4}}\left[46r^{14}+394r^{12}+1027r^{10}+1088r^{8}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad+296r^{6}-370r^{4}-360r^{2}+12r^{4}(r^{2}+2)^{2}(r^{2}+1)^{4}\ln^{2}\left(\frac{2}{r^{2}}+1\right)\right.\\
&\left.\qquad+4(5r^{10}+30r^{8}+73r^{6}+94r^{4}+66r^{2}+20)r^{4}\ln\frac{r_{+}^{2}}{2}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad-2(15r^{14}+154r^{12}+600r^{10}+1190r^{8}+1337r^{6}+876r^{4}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad+316r^{2}+48)r^{2}\ln\left(\frac{2}{r^{2}}+1\right)-96\right].\end{split}

(B.46)

Finally, the solution for
f_{(2,4)}
is given by

\begin{split}f_{(2,4)}(r)&=\frac{c_{4}}{r^{2}}+c_{5}(r^{2}+2)+c_{6}{\gamma}_{+}(r)+c_{7}{\gamma}_{-}(r)-c_{1}-\frac{\ln\frac{r_{+}^{2}}{2}}{16(r^{2}+2)^{2}}\\
&\qquad+\frac{r^{2}\ln^{2}\left(\frac{2}{r^{2}}+1\right)}{32(r^{2}+2)}-\frac{(r^{6}+16r^{4}+48r^{2}+16)\ln\left(\frac{2}{r^{2}}+1\right)}{128(r^{2}+2)^{2}}\\
&\qquad\frac{5r^{8}+38r^{6}+124r^{4}+194r^{2}+80}{128(r^{2}+2)^{3}}\\
&\qquad-\frac{r^{4}}{320(2+r^{2})}\,_{3}F_{2}\left(1,1,3;\frac{7}{2}-\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2},\frac{7}{2}+\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2};\frac{r^{2}}{r^{2}+2}\right).\end{split}

(B.47)

The hypergeometric
\,{}_{3}F_{2}
has a logarithm in its large
r
expansion which must be cancelled in order to impose asymptotically AdS boundary conditions. In particular, this implies that we cannot consistently set
c_{6}=c_{7}=0
anymore which in turn implies non-analytic terms in the near-field expansion as explained in the previous section.

Appendix C
Perturbative construction of hairy solitons and black holes with
A^{1}=A^{2}\equiv A,A^{3}\equiv 0

In this appendix, we present the details of the perturbative construction of the supersymmetric soliton and hairy black holes that were omitted in Section
4.9
of the main text and the explicit solutions to the order that we have derived them.

C.1
Hairy supersymmetric soliton

Recall that we initiate the two charge soliton perturbative construction with the field expansion (
4.49
).
The equation for
\Phi_{(1)}
at
{\cal O}({\epsilon})
is

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dr}\left[\frac{r^{3}}{1+r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}[(1+r^{2})\Phi_{(1)}(r)]\right]&=0.\end{split}

(C.1)

The general solution this is

\begin{split}\Phi_{(1)}(r)=c_{1}\frac{1}{1+r^{2}}+c_{2}\frac{2r^{2}\ln r-1}{r^{2}(1+r^{2})}\end{split}

(C.2)

AdS boundary conditions (
4.46
) imply that
c_{1}=1
and
c_{2}=0
.

At
{\cal O}({\epsilon}^{2})
, the differential equations are

\begin{split}[r^{3}h_{(2)}^{\prime}(r)]^{\prime}&=-\frac{r^{3}}{(1+r^{2})^{2}},\\
[r^{3}[r^{2}f_{(2)}(r)]^{\prime}]^{\prime}&=\frac{2r^{5}(2+r^{2})}{(1+r^{2})^{3}}+16r^{5}h_{(2)}(r),\\
[r^{3}A_{(2)}^{\prime}(r)]^{\prime}&=\frac{r^{3}}{(1+r^{2})^{3}},\\
\left(\frac{1+r^{2}}{r}[r^{2}\varphi_{(2)}(r)]^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}&=-\frac{r}{6(1+r^{2})^{2}}.\\
\end{split}

(C.3)

The general solution is

\begin{split}h_{(2)}(r)&=c_{1}+\frac{c_{2}}{r^{2}}+\frac{1}{8(1+r^{2})},\\
f_{(2)}(r)&=\frac{c_{3}}{r^{2}}+\frac{c_{4}}{r^{4}}+\frac{2c_{1}r^{2}}{3}+2c_{2}+\frac{r^{2}}{4(r^{2}+1)},\\
A_{(2)}(r)&=\mu_{(2)}+\frac{c_{5}}{r^{2}}-\frac{1}{8(r^{2}+1)},\\
\varphi_{(2)}(r)&=\frac{c_{6}}{r^{2}}+c_{7}\frac{\ln(1+r^{2})}{2r^{2}}+\frac{1}{24(1+r^{2})}.\end{split}

(C.4)

AdS boundary conditions implies
c_{1}=c_{7}=0
and regularity at
r=0
fixes
c_{2}=c_{3}=c_{4}=c_{5}=c_{6}=0
.
\mu_{(2)}
is fixed at
{\cal O}({\epsilon}^{3})
.

At
{\cal O}({\epsilon}^{3})
, the differential equation for
\Phi_{(3)}
is

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dr}\left[\frac{r^{3}}{1+r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}[(1+r^{2})\Phi_{(3)}(r)]\right]&=\frac{r^{3}(4-7r^{2}+r^{4})}{2(1+r^{2})^{5}}-\frac{8\mu_{(2)}r^{3}}{(1+r^{2})^{3}}.\end{split}

(C.5)

The general solution to this is

\begin{split}\Phi_{(1)}(r)=c_{1}\frac{1}{1+r^{2}}+c_{2}\frac{2r^{2}\ln r-1}{r^{2}(1+r^{2})}+\frac{1-2r^{2}}{16(1+r^{2})^{3}}-\mu_{(2)}\frac{1+r^{2}\ln(1+\frac{1}{r^{2}})}{r^{2}(1+r^{2})}.\end{split}

(C.6)

AdS boundary conditions sets
c_{1}=c_{2}=0
and regularity at the origin sets
\mu_{(2)}=0
.

Proceeding in this fashion, we can construct the solution to all orders in
{\epsilon}
. The solution to
{\cal O}({\epsilon}^{13})
can be found in the
Mathematica
file.

C.2
Hairy black hole

Recall that we initiate the black hole double expansion perturbative construction with the field expansion (
4.60
), (
4.62
), (
4.63
) and (
4.64
).

General structure of the differential equations at
{\cal O}({\epsilon}^{2n+1}r^{k})

At this order, the only non-trivial equation is for the scalar field. In the far-, intermediate- and near-field regions, the equations take the form

\begin{split}\left[\frac{r^{3}}{1+r^{2}}[(1+r^{2})\Phi_{(2n+1)}(r)]^{\prime}\right]^{\prime}&={\mathscr{S}}^{\Phi}_{(2n+1,k)}(r),\\
[y^{3}[\Phi^{\text{int}}_{(2n+1,k)}(y)]^{\prime}]^{\prime}&={\mathscr{S}}^{\Phi,{\text{int}}}_{(2n+1,k)}(y),\\
[z(z^{2}-1)[\Phi^{\text{near}}_{(2n+1,k)}(z)]^{\prime}]^{\prime}&={\mathscr{S}}^{\Phi,{\text{near}}}_{(2n+1,k)}(z).\\
\end{split}

(C.7)

Each of these differential equations are easily solved up to two integration constants each which are fixed by the AdS boundary condition, regularity on the horizon and matching.

The general solutions for the scalar field at
{\cal O}({\epsilon}r_{+}^{0})
and
{\cal O}({\epsilon}r_{+})
is

\begin{split}\Phi_{{(1,0)}}(r)&=\frac{1}{1+r^{2}},\\
\Phi_{{(1,1)}}(r)&=\frac{1+r^{2}-(5r^{2}+1)\eta_{{(0,0)}}^{2}+r^{2}(r^{2}+1)(\eta_{{(0,0)}}^{2}+1)\ln\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}+1\right)}{2\eta_{{(0,0)}}r^{2}(r^{2}+1)^{2}},\\
\Phi^{\text{int}}_{{(1,0)}}(y)&=a_{1}+\frac{a_{2}}{y^{2}},\\
\Phi^{\text{int}}_{{(1,1)}}(y)&=-a_{1}y^{2}+\frac{a_{2}}{2y^{4}}+a_{3}+\frac{a_{4}}{y^{2}}-\left(a_{2}+a_{1}\eta_{{(0,0)}}-\frac{a_{2}\eta_{{(0,0)}}}{y^{2}}\right)\ln(r_{+}y^{2}),\\
\Phi^{\text{near}}_{{(1,0)}}(z)&=b_{1},\\
\Phi^{\text{near}}_{{(1,1)}}(z)&=b_{2}-2b_{1}\eta_{{(0,0)}}\ln(r_{+}z).\end{split}

(C.8)

The integration constants in the far-field solution have been fixed by asymptotically AdS boundary conditions and one of the integration constants in the near-field solution is fixed by regularity on the horizon.

Consider now the matching of the far- and intermediate-field solutions. Setting
r=\sqrt{r_{+}}y
into the far-field solution and expanding in small
y
, we find

\begin{split}\Phi_{(1)}(\sqrt{r_{+}}y,r_{+})&=\bigg[\frac{1-\eta_{{(0,0)}}^{2}}{2\eta_{{(0,0)}}y^{2}}+1+{\cal O}(y^{2})\bigg]\\
&\qquad+\bigg[-\frac{1+3\eta_{{(0,0)}}^{2}}{2\eta_{{(0,0)}}}-\frac{1+\eta_{{(0,0)}}^{2}}{2\eta_{{(0,0)}}}\ln(r_{+}y^{2})-y^{2}+{\cal O}(y^{4})\bigg]r_{+}\\
&\qquad+{\cal O}(r_{+}^{2})\end{split}

(C.9)

Expanding the intermediate-field solution at large
y
, we find

\begin{split}\Phi^{\text{int}}_{(1)}(y,r_{+})&=\bigg[a_{1}+\frac{a_{2}}{y^{2}}+{\cal O}(y^{-4})\bigg]\\
&\qquad+\bigg[-a_{1}y^{2}+[a_{3}-2(a_{1}\eta_{{(0,0)}}+a_{2})\ln y]+{\cal O}(y^{-2})\bigg]r_{+}+{\cal O}(r_{+}^{2}).\end{split}

(C.10)

Matching the expansions, we find

\begin{split}a_{1}=1,\qquad a_{2}=\frac{1-\eta_{{(0,0)}}^{2}}{2\eta_{{(0,0)}}},\qquad a_{3}=-\frac{1+3\eta_{{(0,0)}}^{2}}{2\eta_{{(0,0)}}}.\end{split}

(C.11)

Next, we turn to the matching of the intermediate-field solution to the near-field solution. To do this, we substitute
y=\sqrt{r_{+}}z
into the intermediate field solution and expand in small
z
,

\begin{split}\Phi^{\text{int}}_{(1)}(\sqrt{r_{+}}z,r_{+})&=\frac{1}{r_{+}}\bigg[\frac{1-\eta_{{(0,0)}}^{2}}{4\eta_{{(0,0)}}z^{4}}+\frac{1-\eta_{{(0,0)}}^{2}}{2\eta_{{(0,0)}}z^{2}}+{\cal O}(z^{4})\bigg]\\
&\qquad+\bigg[\frac{a_{4}+(1-\eta_{{(0,0)}}^{2})\ln(r_{+}z)}{z^{2}}+1+{\cal O}(z^{2})\bigg]\\
&\qquad+\bigg[-\frac{1+3\eta_{{(0,0)}}^{2}+2(1+\eta_{{(0,0)}}^{2})\ln(r_{+}z)}{2\eta_{{(0,0)}}}+{\cal O}(z^{2})\bigg]r_{+}\\
&\qquad+{\cal O}(r_{+}^{2}).\end{split}

(C.12)

Expanding the near-field solution at large
z
, we find

\begin{split}\Phi^{\text{near}}_{(1)}(z,r_{+})&=b_{1}+\bigg[b_{2}-2b_{1}\eta_{{(0,0)}}\ln(r_{+}z)+{\cal O}(z^{-3})\bigg]r_{+}+{\cal O}(r_{+}^{2}).\end{split}

(C.13)

Matching the expansions, we find

\begin{split}\eta_{{(0,0)}}=1,\qquad b_{1}=1,\qquad b_{2}=-2,\qquad a_{4}=0.\end{split}

(C.14)

The rest of the solutions to higher orders is constructed in exactly the same way. The explicit solutions are presented at the end of this section.

General structure of the differential equations at
{\cal O}({\epsilon}^{2n}r^{k})

At this order, we obtain non-trivial differential equations for
f_{(2n,k)}
,
h_{(2n,k)}
,
A_{(2n,k)}
and
\varphi_{(2n,k)}
. The equations in the far-field region are

\begin{split}[r^{3}h_{(2n,k)}^{\prime}(r)]^{\prime}&={\mathscr{S}}^{h}_{(2n,k)}(r),\\
[r^{3}[r^{2}f_{(2n,k)}(r)]^{\prime}]^{\prime}&={\mathscr{S}}^{f}_{(2n,k)}(r)+16r^{5}h_{(2n,k)}(r),\\
[r^{3}A_{(2n,k)}^{\prime}(r)]^{\prime}&={\mathscr{S}}^{A}_{(2n,k)}(r),\\
\left(\frac{1+r^{2}}{r}[r^{2}\varphi_{(2n,k)}(r)]^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}&={\mathscr{S}}^{\varphi}_{(2n,k)}(r).\\
\end{split}

(C.15)

The differential equations in the intermediate-field region take a simpler form if we set

\begin{split}\varphi^{\text{int}}_{(2n,k)}(y)&=X_{(2n,k)}^{\text{int}}(y)+\frac{y^{3}}{4}V_{0}(y)^{2/3}\frac{d}{dy}\bigg(\frac{h_{(2n,k)}^{\text{int}}(y)}{V_{0}(y)^{-2/3}}\bigg),\\
A^{\text{int}}_{(2n,k)}(y)&=Y_{(2n,k)}^{\text{int}}(y)-\frac{X_{(2n,k)}^{\text{int}}(y)}{V_{0}(y)}+\frac{y^{5}}{8}V_{0}(y)^{2}\bigg(\frac{f_{(2n,k)}^{\text{int}}(y)}{y^{2}V_{0}(y)^{2}}\bigg)^{\prime}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{y^{3}}{4}V_{0}(y)\bigg(\frac{h_{(2n,k)}^{\text{int}}(y)}{V_{0}(y)^{2}}\bigg)^{\prime}\end{split}

(C.16)

where
V_{0}(y)=1+y^{-2}
. The equations are then

\begin{split}[X_{(2n,2k)}^{\text{int}}(y)]^{\prime}&={\mathscr{S}}_{(2n,2k)}^{X,{\text{int}}}(y),\\
[Y_{(2n,2k)}^{\text{int}}(y)]^{\prime}&={\mathscr{S}}_{(2n,2k)}^{Y,{\text{int}}}(y),\\
[y^{3}[y^{2}f_{(2n,2k)}^{\text{int}}(y)]^{\prime}]^{\prime}&={\mathscr{S}}_{(2n,2k)}^{f,{\text{int}}}(y),\\
[y^{3}[y^{3}[h_{(2n,k)}^{\text{int}}(y)]^{\prime}]^{\prime}]^{\prime}&={\mathscr{S}}^{h,{\text{int}}}_{(2n,k)}(y)+\frac{1}{2}\bigg(y^{7}V_{0}(y)^{2}\bigg[\frac{[y^{2}f_{(2n,k)}^{\text{int}}(y)]^{\prime}}{y^{3}V_{0}(y)}\bigg]^{\prime}\bigg)^{\prime}\\
&\qquad\qquad-\frac{4}{V_{0}(y)^{2}}\left(y^{3}V_{0}(y)^{2}[X_{(2n,k)}^{\text{int}}(y)]^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}\\
&\qquad\qquad+\frac{4}{V_{0}(y)}\left(y^{3}V_{0}(y)^{2}[Y^{\text{int}}_{(2n,k)}(y)]^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}.\end{split}

(C.17)

Finally, the differential equations in the near-field region take on a simple form if we write

\begin{split}\varphi^{\text{near}}_{(2n,k)}(y)&=X_{(2n,k)}^{\text{near}}(y)+\frac{z^{2}}{3}h_{(2n,k)}^{\text{near}}(y)+\frac{z^{3}}{4}[h_{(2n,k)}^{\text{near}}(y)]^{\prime},\\
h_{(2n,k)}^{\text{near}}(y)&=Y_{(2n,k)}^{\text{near}}(y)-\frac{1}{z^{4}}A_{(2n,k)}^{\text{near}}(y).\end{split}

(C.18)

The equations are then

\begin{split}[X_{(2n,k)}^{\text{near}}(z)]^{\prime}&={\mathscr{S}}_{(2n,k)}^{X,{\text{near}}}(z),\\
[z^{3}[z^{2}Y_{(2n,k)}^{\text{near}}(z)]^{\prime}]^{\prime}&={\mathscr{S}}_{(2n,k)}^{Y,{\text{near}}}(z),\\
[z^{3}[z^{2}f_{(2n,k)}^{\text{near}}(z)]^{\prime}]^{\prime}&={\mathscr{S}}_{(2n,k)}^{f,{\text{near}}}(z),\end{split}

(C.19)

and

\begin{split}{\mathscr{S}}^{A,{\text{near}}}_{(2n,k)}(z)&=\bigg(z(z^{2}-1)^{2}\bigg[\frac{A_{(2n,k)}^{\text{near}}(z)}{z^{2}(z^{2}-1)}\bigg]^{\prime}-4(z^{2}-1)X_{(2n,k)}^{\text{near}}(z)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{z}{2}[z^{2}f_{(2n,k)}^{\text{near}}(z)-2z^{2}(z^{2}-1)Y_{(2n,k)}^{\text{near}}(z)]^{\prime}\bigg)^{\prime}.\end{split}

(C.20)

All the differential equations above can be easily solved. Let us now describe the matching process.

At
{\cal O}({\epsilon}^{2}r_{+}^{0})
, the far-, intermediate- and near-field solutions are given by

\begin{split}h^{\text{far}}_{(2,0)}(r)&=\frac{a_{1}}{r^{2}}+\frac{1}{8(1+r^{2})},\\
f^{\text{far}}_{(2,0)}(r)&=\frac{a_{2}}{r^{2}}+a_{1}\left(2+\frac{4}{r^{2}}\right)+\frac{r^{2}}{4(1+r^{2})},\\
A^{\text{far}}_{(2,0)}(r)&=a_{3}+\frac{a_{4}}{r^{2}}-\frac{1}{8(1+r^{2})},\\
\varphi^{\text{far}}_{(2,0)}(r)&=\frac{a_{1}}{3r^{2}}+\frac{1}{24(1+r^{2})},\\
X_{(2,0)}^{\text{int}}(y)&=b_{1},\\
Y_{(2,0)}^{\text{int}}(y)&=b_{2},\\
f_{(2,0)}^{\text{int}}(y)&=\frac{b_{3}}{y^{2}}+\frac{b_{4}}{y^{4}},\\
h_{(2,0)}^{\text{int}}(y)&=\frac{b_{4}}{6y^{6}}+\frac{b_{5}}{y^{4}}+\frac{b_{6}}{y^{2}}+b_{7},\\
X_{(2,0)}^{\text{near}}(z)&=c_{1},\\
Y_{(2,0)}^{\text{near}}(z)&=\frac{c_{2}}{z^{2}}+\frac{c_{3}}{z^{4}},\\
f_{(2,0)}^{\text{near}}(z)&=-2(2c_{1}+c_{2}-c_{3})\frac{z^{2}-1}{z^{4}},\\
A_{(2,0)}^{\text{near}}(z)&=-\frac{1}{2}(z^{2}-1)(4c_{1}+2c_{2}+c_{4}z^{2}).\end{split}

(C.21)

Here, we have used AdS boundary conditions and regularity at the horizon in addition to the requirement
f^{\text{near}}(1)=A^{\text{near}}(1)=0
to fix some of the integration constants. The remaining ones are fixed by matching.

Setting
r=\sqrt{r_{+}}y
in the far-field solution and expanding at small
r_{+}
and
y
, we find

\begin{split}f_{(2)}(\sqrt{r_{+}}y,r_{+})&=\frac{1}{r_{+}}\left[\frac{4a_{1}+a_{2}}{y^{2}}+{\cal O}(y^{2})\right]+2a_{1}+{\cal O}(r_{+}),\\
h_{(2)}(\sqrt{r_{+}}y,r_{+})&=\frac{1}{r_{+}}\left[\frac{a_{1}}{y^{2}}+{\cal O}(y^{2})\right]+\frac{1}{8}+{\cal O}(r_{+}),\\
A_{(2)}(\sqrt{r_{+}}y,r_{+})&=\frac{1}{r_{+}}\left[\frac{a_{4}}{y^{2}}+{\cal O}(y^{2})\right]+a_{3}-\frac{1}{8}+{\cal O}(r_{+}),\\
\varphi_{(2)}(\sqrt{r_{+}}y,r_{+})&=\frac{1}{r_{+}}\left[\frac{a_{1}}{3y^{2}}+{\cal O}(y^{2})\right]+\frac{1}{24}+{\cal O}(r_{+}).\\
\end{split}

(C.22)

We next expand the intermediate field solution at small
r_{+}
and large
y
,

\begin{split}f^{\text{int}}_{(2)}(y,r_{+})&={\cal O}(y^{-2})+{\cal O}(r_{+}),\\
h^{\text{int}}_{(2)}(y,r_{+})&=b_{7}+{\cal O}(r_{+}),\\
A^{\text{int}}_{(2)}(y,r_{+})&=-b_{1}+b_{2}-\frac{b_{3}}{2}+\frac{b_{6}}{2}-b_{7}+{\cal O}(r_{+}),\\
\varphi^{\text{int}}_{(2)}(y,r_{+})&=b_{1}-\frac{b_{6}}{2}+\frac{b_{7}}{3}+{\cal O}(r_{+}).\end{split}

(C.23)

Matching the two expansions, we find

\begin{split}a_{1}=a_{2}=a_{4}=0,\qquad a_{3}=b_{2}-\frac{b_{3}}{2},\qquad b_{6}=2b_{1},\qquad b_{7}=\frac{1}{8}.\end{split}

(C.24)

To match the intermediate-field solution to the near-field one, we set
y=\sqrt{r_{+}}z
in the intermediate-field solution and expand at small
r_{+}
and
z
and we find

\begin{split}f_{(2)}^{\text{int}}(\sqrt{r_{+}}z,r_{+})&=\frac{1}{r_{+}^{2}}\left[\frac{b_{4}}{z^{4}}+{\cal O}(z^{2})\right]+\frac{1}{r_{+}}\left[\frac{b_{3}}{z^{2}}+{\cal O}(z^{2})\right]+{\cal O}(r_{+}),\\
h_{(2)}^{\text{int}}(\sqrt{r_{+}}z,r_{+})&=\frac{1}{r_{+}^{3}}\left[\frac{b_{4}}{6z^{6}}+{\cal O}(z^{2})\right]+\frac{1}{r_{+}^{2}}\left[\frac{b_{5}}{z^{4}}+{\cal O}(z^{2})\right]\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{r_{+}}\left[\frac{2b_{1}}{z^{2}}+{\cal O}(z^{2})\right]+{\cal O}(r_{+}^{0}),\\
A_{(2)}^{\text{int}}(\sqrt{r_{+}}z,r_{+})&=\frac{1}{r_{+}}\left[-\frac{b_{4}}{6z^{2}}+{\cal O}(z^{2})\right]+b_{2}-\frac{5b_{4}}{12}\\
&\qquad+\left[\frac{1}{4}(-8b_{1}-2b_{3}+b_{4}+4b_{5})z^{2}+{\cal O}(z^{4})\right]r_{+}+{\cal O}(r_{+}^{2}),\\
\varphi_{(2)}^{\text{int}}(\sqrt{r_{+}}z,r_{+})&=\frac{1}{r_{+}^{2}}\left[-\frac{7b_{4}}{36z^{4}}+{\cal O}(z^{2})\right]+\frac{1}{r_{+}}\left[\frac{1}{z^{2}}\left(-\frac{b_{4}}{18}-\frac{2b_{5}}{3}\right)+{\cal O}(z^{2})\right]\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{18}(12b_{1}+b_{4}-6b_{5})+{\cal O}(r_{+}).\\
\end{split}

(C.25)

Finally, we expand the near-field solution at small
r_{+}
and large
z
,

\begin{split}f_{(2)}^{\text{near}}(z,r_{+})&=\left[-\frac{2(2c_{1}+c_{2}-c_{3})}{z^{2}}+{\cal O}(z^{-4})\right]+{\cal O}(r_{+}),\\
h_{(2)}^{\text{near}}(z,r_{+})&=\frac{1}{r_{+}}\left[\frac{c_{4}}{2}+\frac{4(c_{1}+c_{2})-c_{4}}{2z^{2}}+{\cal O}(z^{-4})\right]+{\cal O}(r_{+}^{0}),\\
A_{(2)}^{\text{near}}(z,r_{+})&=\left[-\frac{c_{4}z^{4}}{2}+\frac{1}{2}(-4c_{1}-2c_{2}+c_{4})z^{2}+(2c_{1}+c_{2})+{\cal O}(z^{-2})\right]r_{+}\\
&\qquad+{\cal O}(r_{+}^{2}),\\
\varphi_{(2)}^{\text{near}}(z,r_{+})&=\left[\frac{c_{4}}{6}z^{2}+\frac{1}{12}(8c_{1}-4c_{2}+c_{4})+{\cal O}(z^{-2})\right]+{\cal O}(r_{+}).\\
\end{split}

(C.26)

The expansions are matched by setting

\begin{split}b_{1}=c_{1},\qquad b_{2}=b_{3}=b_{4}=b_{5}=c_{2}=c_{4}=0,\qquad c_{3}=2c_{1}+x_{(2,0)}.\end{split}

(C.27)

We find that the matching procedure fixes all constants except
x_{(2,0)}
and
c_{1}
. The former is fixed by the matching procedure at
{\cal O}(r_{+}^{2})
which sets

\begin{split}x_{(2,0)}=0.\end{split}

(C.28)

On the other hand,
c_{1}
is a redundant parameter as the full hairy BH solution only depends on the combination
\eta_{(2,0)}+2c_{1}
. We can then set
c_{1}=0
without loss of generality.

This procedure can be continued on to any order in perturbation theory. The explicit solution can be found in the accompanying
Mathematica
file.
